Quantitative Equity Research

# Quant Matters – Capturing Mid Cap Opportunities With Caution

Intense market concentration has led to growing attention on spaces beyond large caps. Mid caps tend to outperform large caps as inflation stabilises and rate cuts start. Value and Quality are the top performing styles in mid caps, from which we construct a Mid Cap Composite long only factor.

QUANTWISE 图

## Key Takeaways

Specific risks are converging across all sizes (large, mid and small), indicating comparable risk levels across sizes.

Mid caps tend to outperform large caps and small caps as inflation stabilises following moderation, as well as in the early phase of rate cuts.

Value and Quality are the top performing styles in mid cap for both regions in the long term as well as in the past three years.

– Actual earnings growth, unlike expected growth, is lower as we move from large to small size cohorts. We prefer Low Growth for mid caps vs. usual perception.

■ Our Mid Cap Composite is tilted to Quality Value, focusing on cash flow generation from strong operations, and cash preservation against aggressive expansion.

The 4 Dimensions Update (framework in this report) & Factor Views: Europe – Net Buyback Yield (+), Total Yield (+), FCF Yield (+). The rankings of factor subgroups in January moved in comparison to December. Composite Momentum factor ranking fell to 10 (from 1 in December). On the other hand, Defensive Value and Composite Growth rankings improved. Quality rankings remain similar to last month.

US – Net Buyback Yield (+), Profitability (+), Low PEG (+). In the US, rankings in January are fairly similar to December with a few exceptions. Composite Value and Growth rankings improved. Leverage and Revisions rankings dropped. Composite Quality is now ranked best in the framework. Low Vol, Momentum and Growth factors continue to rank low.

Factor performance in January: In January, factor performances were mixed with Composite Momentum, Growth and Low Volatility factors gaining while Size (Small) underperformed. Low Vol gained back some of its December loss by moving up by 6% in Europe (3% in the US). Composite Momentum and Growth gained 5% each in Europe (7% and 3% respectively in the US). Size (Small) fell -4% in each region while Value and Quality were close to flat or gaining close to 1%. Total Yield climbed 2% in Europe and 1% in the US.

## Capturing Mid Cap Opportunities with Caution

2023 was a year of outperformance for large caps (particularly the Magnificent 7), while performance of mid caps was relatively muted. Given the concentration of the market index in the US and Europe, investors have attempted to seek alpha opportunities beyond the Magnificent 7 and large caps. As such, this month we focus on the Size dimension of stocks in the US and Europe.

For the purpose of this section, we rank the MSCI US/Europe stocks by market cap and refer to top 45% by number of stocks as 'large caps', and the remaining stocks as 'mid caps'. We do not specify the threshold percentage based on market caps but number of stocks due to intensifying index concentration. At present MSCI Europe and US Mid Cap stocks form close to 55% of the standard MSCI universes, hence we use this percentage in our definition as well. We refer to constituents of MSCI US/Europe Small Cap Indices as 'small caps'. The performances are measured on equal-weighted basis in this section, unless otherwise specified.

## Why mid caps are gaining attention now

Long-term outperformance of mid caps vs. large caps: Since 2000s, mid caps have outperformed large caps in both the US and Europe, with much more sizeable outperformance in the US. Despite long-term outperformance, risk appetite for mid caps reduces during times of stress. Since 2020, performance of large caps has surpassed mid caps in both regions. While the recent strong US economic data, including aboveexpectation GDP, payrolls and tight labour market, have cooled down, the market expectations of rate cuts in March this year (vs. June as forecast by our economists), an eventual reduction in interest rates later this year could ease funding costs. Mid caps and even small caps would potentially expect more tailwinds from the interest burden perspective.

Exhibit 2 to Exhibit 5 show the equal-weighted performances of MSCI Europe and US universes by size. In the US, mid caps outperform large caps long term (see Exhibit 2 ). That said, performance since COVID-19 has shown a shift in investors' preference to large caps. Of note, mid caps and small caps in the US performed rather similarly (see Exhibit 3 ).

Exhibit 2: US: Mid caps have outperformed large caps since 2000.
![](images/c70028db8dba915f2456f1f40ce5febf64bf0fb79aba76da5f675ad1477f6b00.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. Performance measured on equal-weighted basis

Exhibit 3: US: Since Dec 2019, large caps have started to outperform mid caps.
![](images/b3ab309e0af3caaf8ad5a9aab339584d13acc171aa9c336a23ea0db5e89cc389.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. Performance measured on equal-weighted basis

In Europe, mid caps have slightly outperformed large caps since 2000. Unlike in the US, small caps in Europe have outperformed the other two size cohorts by notable margins. The trend appears to cease since 2020, when the relative performance of the three size cohorts in Europe becomes rather similar to the US (see Exhibit 5 ).

Exhibit 4: Europe: Mid caps have outperformed large caps since 2000, while small caps have delivered notably better performance.
![](images/4e5306dcf1ac2df5130dbb81d678c919b5ee3735468b4fb201ce336a71f4f0e0.jpg)

Exhibit 5: Europe: Since Dec 2019, large caps have started to outperform mid caps and small caps.
![](images/0177d574a9e1737fb2f040348b67f342bb5f6aacbf54286ba19b17e31e48a925.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. Performance measured on equal-weighted basis
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. Performance measured on equal-weighted basis

Market concentration weighing on overall performance since 2020: We would highlight the impact of market concentrations in both the US and Europe since 2020. In particular, while large caps have done better than mid caps and small caps, much of the outperformance is contributed by the Magnificent 7 in 2022. Indeed, if we remove the Magnificent 7 from large cap cohorts in the US, its cap-weighted performance is similar to that of mid cap (see Exhibit 6 ). Since Dec-19, mid caps have delivered weaker performance, but the performance is not very different from large caps on a cap-weighted basis.

Exhibit 6: US: When we exclude Magnificent 7 from large caps, the cap-weighted performance is very close to that of mid caps.
![](images/b4cf4c68ffcdb2a1b851e911c58b1baf00a3a49b9c061519dbcab159317577bc.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

Unbiased pick-up in market confidence: The market also seems to bet against mid caps trending lower today compared to one year ago. Exhibit 7 and Exhibit 8 measure the median utilisation ratio, defined as the balance between the demand and supply of stock loan, of all the stocks in each size cohort per region. The utlilisation ratio has been reducing across all size cohorts over the past year. This suggests that the demand for shorting has not only reduced for large caps, but for mid caps (and small caps) as well. Market confidence seems to be unbiased regardless of the stock sizes.

Exhibit 7: US: Utilisation ratio at mid cap level has been reducing over the last one year.
![](images/20f980458c865495f08c63779a2eff3773ac09b859f59287e2c68cb3437d6382.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. The Utilisation Ratio measures the percentage of supply being covered by the demand, and is calculated as Short interest / Actively available amount of stock loan supply. The active element refers to IHS Markit adjustments for small or inactive inventory.

Exhibit 8: Europe: Utilisation ratio at mid cap level has been reducing over the last one year.
![](images/cefc60c73f857a89bc2c0a97ecf4d975f09b8215a31b484c50a2cb67a5d51c3d.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. The Utilisation Ratio measures the percentage of supply being covered by the demand, and is calculated as Short interest / Actively available amount of stock loan supply. The active element refers to IHS Markit adjustments for small or inactive inventory.

Converging specific risks across sizes: In Exhibit 9 and Exhibit 10 , we perform regression of 63-day stock return adjusting for the effects of market, size, quality, price momentum, valuation, and sector. We obtain the regression residual for each stock, and calculate the median residual per size cohort. The specific risk in mid cap universe has been declining over the past year in both regions, now to the level close to those exhibited by large caps. This suggests similar opportunity sets for stock picking and comparable risk levels across sizes.

Exhibit 9: US: Specific risks across sizes are converging.
![](images/f7fef4aa3cfc4d06f54829f9d1127384780c834358b1f4fb5945fca773849d56.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 10: Europe: Specific risk level across sizes are converging.
![](images/bd29a66d5d4ac7ae5fe7e2141f210643adee62487d1fc159ff8d8cd90d737cc2.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

Similar sector exposures across size cohorts: In the US, Financials, Health Care, IT and Industrials are the top sectors in each of the three size cohorts (see Exhibit 11 ). In Europe, Financials and Industrials make up the top concentrated sectors across all sizes. Of note, Financials stocks in Europe make up 22% of large cap stocks, but only 11% for small cap stocks (see Exhibit 12 ).

Exhibit 11: US: Financials, Health Care, IT and Industrials are the top sectors when looked at by any size.
![](images/59314b988bfbef71f8862592540465844077a7fdfde79133b09aa334e0a54620.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. Data as of end-Jan 2024.

Exhibit 12: Europe: Financials and Industrials make up the top concentrated sectors across all sizes.
![](images/2b6aa070d4f88543f7eeab591c5c73e554a99a2a60c9e5f6d9cbd1566017b562.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. Data as of end-Jan 2024.

Valuation of mid caps does not appear demanding vs. large caps: Historically, mid caps tend to trade at lower valuation than large caps. Currently, there is a large divergence in valuation (in terms of P/B) between large and mid caps in the US (see Exhibit 13 ) – over the long-term, large caps in the US trade at 3.3x P/B on average, vs. 5.0x currently. In contrast, the US mid caps trade at 2.3x P/B on average over the long term, vs. 2.8x currently. In Europe, large caps trade at 2.1x P/B on average (and at the same level for now), and mid caps trade at 1.7x P/B on average, vs. 1.6x P/B currently (see Exhibit 14 ).

We would highlight that the valuation of large caps appears to be much more volatile than mid caps or small caps. Historically, convergence of the valuation gap between large caps and mid caps is mainly driven by valuation correction or derating in large caps.

Exhibit 13: US: Valuation spread is currently wide between large and mid caps.
![](images/1f8be661684e38061fd8058506c7588820638bfa2eadd6a80245c070d647379c.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 14: Europe: Mid caps are cheaper than large cap at the moment, but the spread is not as wide as in the US.
![](images/a1d1b158b4c90f3d72747633bd3d92956563409c6b09cf662f6676a47b01b975.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

## Performance Of Size By Macro Regimes

Generally speaking, the relative performance of small caps vs. large caps reflects the market sentiment, and this induces us to study the impact of macro regimes on the performance of the three size cohorts in each region. For instance, small caps may be more susceptible to funding challenges amidst rising in interest rates, so they could underperform large caps in an environment of rising interest rate. Here we focus on the two most topical macro regimes at the moment, namely inflation levels and interest rates. Specifically, we review the overall performance of each size cohort and the overall market under falling and stable inflation regimes, as well as higher for longer interest rates and rates cut afterwards.

Mid caps could outperform large and small caps the most as inflation stabilises following moderation. In general, the market (MSCI US or Europe) delivers better performance when inflation is stable vs. on a falling trend, since falling inflation is generally associated with slowdown in the economy, particularly when there is concern of deflation. In such cases, large caps outperform mid caps and small caps in both regions. When inflation stabilises from a falling inflation regime, the market performance improves notably. In particular, the improvement in performance is the most sizeable for mid caps - the Sharpe ratio improves from 0.67 to 1.39 in the US and from 0.58 to 1.05 in Europe, vs. 0.70 to 1.24 for the US large caps and 0.69 to 1.00 for the European large caps. Inflation is currently moderating in both the US and Europe and moving towards the central banks' targets. Hence inflation would be transitioning from 'falling' to 'stable' later this year, which mid caps may leverage for outperformance (see Exhibit 15 and Exhibit 16 ).

Exhibit 15: US: Mid caps outperform other sizes during stable inflationary periods.
![](images/3c1d9db62196e06bed5e3bea0134c9430c0c71a81ebd649ffaf9a3afc7a80785.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 16: Europe: Mid caps outperform other sizes during stable inflationary periods.
![](images/58d59637d87e16ab62a9d82a1e6d3b142b7ad99fe08c8d42641e17326f1ab855.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

Mid caps could outperform large and small caps in the early phase of rate cuts. On absolute basis, the impact of interest rate regime on the US and Europe markets is rather different. However, on relative basis, mid caps underperform the markets and large caps when interest rates stay higher for longer, and the underperformance is even larger for small caps. We believe this is due to stronger position to request or retain credit by large caps during the period of more expensive funding costs. However, as it transitions to the rate cuts period post higher for longer regimes, mid caps start to outperform the market and large caps, although the back-test Sharpe ratios are not extraordinary (see Exhibit 17 and Exhibit 18 ). Mid caps, particularly in the US, are more leveraged than large caps in the long term, thus they are more vulnerable to high rates and in turn also benefit more from rate cuts.

Exhibit 17: US: Mid caps do better during periods of rate cuts although the Sharpe ratio is weak.
![](images/5c0dc62e315b891f2ea9296e790f20a292a64e963d3de6e2062e0bab0e28cd71.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

Exhibit 18: Europe: Mid caps do better during periods of rate cuts but at weak Sharpe ratio, similar to the US.
![](images/f75d45b67083de09b0fc295c9c1d498ea84cf0a8bcfdf6f3336bd69959f43b80.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

From the analyses above, performance of mid caps relative to other size cohorts could be quite different depending on the macro regimes. Further, valuation itself may not suffice to justify the potential upcoming out/underperformance of certain size cohort – for instance, large valuation dispersion between mid caps and large caps, particularly in the US, would not necessarily be associated with re-rating of mid caps. As such, we take another perspective to study the factor performance within each size cohort and explore what factors would potentially be more favourable for mid caps.

## Factors By Size

To start with, we calculate the long-short Sharpe ratios of the key factors or styles by size cohorts for the US in Exhibit 19 . We make a few key observations:

• Value and Quality factors have positive efficacy across all size cohorts both in the long term and over the past three years. These two well documented factors are insensitive to market size. In particular, Value factor performs particularly well for small caps over the past three years.

• Growth factor hardly provides useful signals in the long term across all size cohorts. In the past three years, the market seems to prefer low growth for large and mid caps, although it has a certain amount of efficacy in small caps.

• In the long term, Low Volatility factor does not serve as useful signal, although it starts to become efficacious in mid and small caps space over the past three years. Despite the perceived higher risk overall for these two size cohorts, the unprecedented condensed market cycles in the past three years reward exposures to Low Volatility.

• We also highlight the emergence of efficacy of the Momentum factors for mid and small caps in the past three years. This shift is primarily driven by Revisions, which has shown >1 Sharpe ratio in the last 3 years in mid caps, while Price Momentum is relatively weaker.

Exhibit 19: US: Value and Quality tend to do well across sizes, while other factors are more mixed by size/period of backtest.

## Value vs. Growth by Size

We noted earlier that mid caps are positively exposed to Value in terms of factor performance, while Growth does not show strong efficacy over both the long term and near term. Expanding this further, Exhibit 21 and Exhibit 22 show the 10-year performance trajectories of the Value and Growth factors within mid caps in both regions. It appears the Value exposure in mid caps is well leveraged to the cyclical recovery post recession due to the pandemic since 2021.

Exhibit 21: US: Value performance has been on the rise while Growth remained flat.
![](images/853e7717c3feb9c91532378256832fa99d2efbb7d77f5f4eb10e46557cd2412d.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 22: Europe: Value performance has been on the rise while Growth fell.
![](images/6ecba48ab0e3f4a4bfc1fe13455e995386a31d342e4772bf8daa723aa868c1fd.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

Smaller caps are often considered higher growth potential, which contrasts against the long-term underperformance of the Growth factors within both mid and small caps to large caps. Hence we study in more detail the delivery of growth within each size cohort. In particular, within large, mid and small cap universes respectively, we calculate the median forecast earnings growth in FY2 vs. FY1, as well as the median actual annual earnings growth in each month, then calculate the long-term average since 2000 ( Exhibit 23 and Exhibit 24 ).

In both regions, the forecast earnings growth is similar for large and mid caps, but it is much higher for small caps. This is in line with the expectation of higher growth potentials particularly for small caps. However, while large caps manage to deliver actual earnings growth to largely meet the market expectation, mid and small caps' actual earnings growth fall short of the expectation in the long-run – this is in line with the finding of Hou and van Dijk (2010) that there is significantly negative (positive) profitability shocks to small (big) firms in the US and Europe. Indeed, the actual earnings growth is actually

lower as we move from large to small size cohorts. Essentially it explains why practitioners like Growth while academics say it does not exist: academics look at the whole market while many practitioners concentrate on large caps.

In addition, we also calculate the standard deviation of actual earnings growth in each month and plot the long-term average in green dots. The growth profiles become more uncertain, as indicated by higher standard deviation, as we move from large to small size cohorts. For these two reasons, we would prefer low growth exposures for mid and small caps, in contrary to the usual perception of high growth.

Exhibit 23: US: Actual earnings growth gets notably lower and more dispersed as we move from large size to small size cohorts.
![](images/dbc9e426597003b792818889ce775157988bb0ddda9879d4dbd41de2290dc044.jpg)
Forecast earnings growth Actual earnings growth Actual earnings growth dispersion (RHS)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 24: Europe: Actual earnings growth gets notably lower and more dispersed as we move from large size to small size cohorts.
![](images/8f2d65e4d09fea68414418a98273529815620c9d2abcaf00d5af2ab61feb97b0.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

## Top/bottom Factors, By Size

While previous analyses on the key styles have highlighted the efficaciousness of Value and Quality within mid caps, we expand the analysis to all 58 factors we have been tracking. Exhibit 25 shows the top/bottom five out of the 58 factors based on back-test performance since 1990. We also perform the same analysis for large and small caps for comparison. We highlight a few key findings:

• Low Accruals is the best performing factor for both regions. Two out of the top five factors belong to Quality family in Europe, and four in the US.

• Cash flow related factors are another spotlight among the top five factors. All the Value factors in the top five spots are related to strong cash flow generation.

• Sales and earnings growth are key components of the bottom five factors for mid caps in both regions. Indeed, we draw similar conclusion for small caps as well.

## Mid Cap Composite Factor

In the final section, we combine our insights from the earlier two sections to create a composite factor for mid caps that can be considered as a starting point to incrementally gain mid cap exposures. To recap, we firstly analyse that mid caps outperform other size cohorts amidst stable inflation and periods of rate cuts post higher for longer regime. But mid caps do not outperform in all relevant regimes today. Hence, we analyse each size cohort through the factor lens for more resilient long-term performance. We find that Value rather than Growth drives outperformance of mid caps, and Quality has quite high long-term efficacy as well.

We bring together these findings to create Mid Cap Composite factor. The composite factor comprises two Value, two Quality and one factor each from Revisions and Low Growth. Preferences go to cheap valuation, high quality, low earnings growth, and good revisions (see Exhibit 26 ).

Within Value, we focus on the yield factors emphasising cash flow generation, namely free cash flow yield and total yield. We also prefer mid caps with more conservative stance through high earnings quality (low accruals) and management quality (low assets growth). The low growth preference is augmented by our explicit preference of low earnings growth factor, which we modify from our usual 58-factor framework to account for the learning from Exhibit 23 and Exhibit 24 . We also refer to strong EPS revisions to leverage the near-term sentiment on the individual mid cap stocks. In summary, our mid cap factor preferences are tilted to Quality Value with emphasis on cash flow generation from strong operation, as well as cash preservation against aggressive or even excessive expansion.

Exhibit 26: Mid Cap Long Only Composite Factor
![](images/2f81ba54263c469de3f569b28d9aeed210a6aac9f8cd88028c2c8d21f25cf5d2.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

We summarise the back-test result of Mid Cap Composite factor and its components in terms of long term Sharpe ratios in Exhibit 27 , based on the long-only performance of the top quintile for each factor. Mid Cap Composite Factor does better than most of its individual components and the market. Long term, the Sharpe ratio stands at 0.76 in the US and 0.65 in Europe. In the past three years, while the performance in Europe has deteriorated (0.22 Sharpe), it delivers comparable performance in the US (0.71 Sharpe). The composite has beaten market (overall mid caps) in both periods for both region.

Here we focus on the top quintile of the composite factor for a long-only strategy, since shorting mid caps could be more costly than large caps or the overall index. If we pursue for a long-short strategy based on this composite factor, the pre-cost Sharpe ratio since 1990 is 0.40 for the US, and 0.89 for Europe.

Exhibit 27: Long-only Sharpe ratio of Mid Cap Composite Factor and its components.
<table><tr><td colspan="5">US Mid Cap Europe Mid Cap</td></tr><tr><td>Group</td><td>Factor/Index</td><td>LT 3Y</td><td>LT</td><td>3Y</td></tr><tr><td>Revisions</td><td>EPS Rev Ratio 3ma Accruals*</td><td>0.75 0.74 0.74 0.42</td><td>0.60 0.66</td><td>0.15 0.46</td></tr><tr><td>Quality</td><td>Asset Gr*</td><td>0.67 0.37</td><td>0.50</td><td>0.23</td></tr><tr><td>Value</td><td>Total Yield FCF Yield</td><td>0.74 0.61 0.77 0.48</td><td>0.67 0.54</td><td>0.39 0.19</td></tr><tr><td>Growth</td><td>Low Earnings Growth</td><td>0.63 0.47</td><td>0.31</td><td>0.15</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Composite</td><td>0.76 0.71</td><td>0.65</td><td>0.22</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Mid cap Index</td><td>0.68</td><td>0.31 0.44</td><td>0.09</td></tr></table>

The valuation, measured as the median forward P/E vs. mid cap market, of Mid Cap Composite factor is also relatively cheap, with Europe at 30th percentile (since 1990) and the US at 14th percentile (see Exhibit 28 ), contributed by inclusion of cheap valuation into the composite factor.

Exhibit 28: Valuation of Mid Cap Composite factor remains cheap in both regions.
![](images/9447c935d9760644bb9cfaf98ee2058be3f8d5bde937ae8d43786f58df476ca7.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

## Composite By Macro Regimes

Finally, we back-test the performance of Mid Cap Composite factor in the most relevant regimes discussed earlier, and we show the results in Exhibit 29 and Exhibit 30 . We recall that mid caps tend to outperform large caps and small caps when the inflation is stable and during the early phase of rates cut regime. Mid Cap Composite factor further outperforms the overall mid caps in these two regimes.

Exhibit 29: US: Mid cap composite outperforms all sizes and bench during stable inflationary period.
![](images/f6eef2a15b2ac2a73792a1702e8e06e5ece7f648d2cd2501cf6bc56ca529dcbd.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 30: Europe: Mid cap composite outperforms all sizes and bench during stable inflationary period.

![](images/b6e15c2686de1f6e4fbf8b4da48163c3932c46921107a684e375c56160be02bd.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

## Conclusion

More intense market concentration has led to growing investor interest in seeking opportunities beyond the Magnificent 7 and large caps. Mid caps have outperformed large caps in long term, although such outperformance has been suppressed in recent few years. However, mid caps tend to outperform large caps and small caps during early periods of rate cuts or stable inflation regime which our economists forecast to take place later during the year. That said, mid caps may still face headwinds under the current backdrop. As such, we study the long-term factor behaviours of mid caps, and find that Value, Quality and strong Revisions are useful in identifying long-term winners relative to other mid caps. We highlight that cash preservation is more important against aggressive or even excessive expansion and prefer low growth, in contrary to usual perception, since mid caps (and also small caps) tend to fall short of earnings growth expectations in the long-run. We also create Mid Cap Composite factor which further outperforms the overall mid caps, based on our back-test.

## Reference

Hou, Kewei and van Dijk, Mathijs A. (2010): "Profitability Shocks and the Size Effect in the Cross-Section of Expected Stock Returns". Available at SSRN.

## Europe: The 4 Dimensions update

The rankings of factor subgroups in January moved in comparison to December. Composite Momentum factor ranking fell to 10 (from 1 in December) in a month due to expensive valuation, increased ERR and overbought signals from price z-score. On the other hand, Defensive Value and Composite Growth rankings improved on the back of stronger 1Y performance and reduced ERR. Quality rankings remain similar to last month. Our preferred factors are Net Buyback Yield, Total Yield and FCF Yield.

## US: The 4 Dimensions update

In the US, rankings in January are fairly similar to December with a few exceptions. Composite Value rankings improved from cheaper valuation while Growth improved from stronger 1Y efficacy and lower ERR. Leverage and Revisions rankings dropped from expensive valuation and increased ERR. Lastly, Composite Quality is now ranked best in the framework. Low Vol, Momentum and Growth factors continue to rank low. Our factor recommendations are Net Buyback Yield, Profitability, and Low PEG.

## Defensive Value

In Europe, Defensive Value factor jumped to 7th position in our Framework from stronger 1Y performance and lower ERR. We maintain our preference for this factor strategically, as yield factor tends to outperform in a tightened monetary condition, given the defensive nature. This thematic factor comprises Total Yield and Dividend Yield.

## Net Buyback Yield

This factor ranks best (1st of 58) in our Framework. The factor has strong performance and cheap valuations. The volume of net buyback remains high, currently close to 97th percentile since 1990.

The key drivers are i) high long term and 5- year efficacies, and ii) cheap valuation. A key risk could be that companies that have strong (historical) buybacks could change course unexpectedly and stop their buyback program. (Quantitative Equity Research: Market – Factor Chartbook, 16 Aug 2022). We are closely monitoring the trend of buyback volume to assess such risks.

Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/aea45b75e8768464f2e7c3cbee8587037f44ab426a759566816dabe40bdb0806.jpg)

## FCF Yield

Europe's FCF Yield ranks 6th (of 58) in our framework. It is attractive, with strong efficacy in the long run and cheap valuation.

## US Region Factor Views

Exhibit 36: Net Buyback Yield
![](images/fc096bd0bf218527c7524feb56b4d57cc93c532040a7049f806e07e99a6b0d17.jpg)

## Net Buyback Yield

This factor is in 7th position (of 58) in our Framework. The factor has strong performance, cheap valuations and good exposure to the current economic regime. The volume of net buybacks has reduced but remains high, currently at 95th percentile since 1990.

The key drivers are i) strong long term and 5-year efficacies, ii) positive performance during rising yield environment, and iii) cheap valuation. Similar to Europe, a key risk could be that companies with strong (historical) buybacks could change course unexpectedly and stop their buyback program (Quantitative Equity Research: Market – Factor Chartbook, 16 Aug 2022). We are closely monitoring the trend of buyback volume to assess such risks. The current macro environment should continue to favour this factor as one of the key Defensive factors.

## Profitability

In the US, the Profitability subgroup ranks 14th in our framework. This thematic factor includes ROE, ROIC, Net Margin, EBITDA Margin, GPOA and ROA signals. We continue to recommend this factor on the continued need for monitoring healthy earnings.

The key drivers are i) strong 5Y and 1Y efficacies, and ii) low ERR.

## Low PEG

In the US, Low PEG ranks 1st (of 58) in our framework. The factor has cheap valuations. We believe it would be more prudent to gain exposure to earnings growth at reasonable valuation (GARP) vs. expensive valuation in many other Growth factors before more signs of sustained economic growth emerge.

The key drivers are i) strong long term efficacy and ii) favourable macro backdrop across all four macro variables we monitor in the framework.

## Factor performance in January

In January, factor performances were mixed with Composite Momentum, Growth and Low Volatility factor gaining while Size (Small) underperforming. Low Vol gained back some of its December loss by moving up by 6% in Europe (3% in the US). Composite Momentum and Growth gained 5% each in Europe (7% and 3% respectively in the US). Size (Small) fell -4% in each region while Value and Quality were close to flat or gaining close to 1%. Total Yield climbed 2% in Europe and 1% in the US.

Value gained sharply over the first week in both regions after FOMC Minutes indicated restrictive policy and rates remaining higher for longer. Post first week, value started to drop with January ending close to flat. Growth on the other hand fell in the first week but later had a steady gain through January (5% Europe, 3% US). The valuation of Value remains low, below the 15th percentile over the 30Y history in both regions. We prefer Defensive Value in Europe as it is well exposed to Recession regime and tends to be resilient in a still-high rate regime. While we maintain our preference for defensive exposures in the US, we recommend incrementally adding exposure to Growth at reasonable valuation through Low PEG factor.

Momentum gained 5% in Europe and 7% in the US. Momentum has become expensive in Europe this month with valuation percentile jumping to 49th percentile. In the US, the factor remains expensive at 91%-ile. After 3 months of negative performance (August - October 2023) in the US and Europe, the equity market has rotated since November, with MSCI US and Europe moving a little over 15% each between November and January. Momentum factor may remain volatile with such trade reversals.

Quality grew 1% in the US and remained flat in Europe. In Europe, Quality factor is very expensive – our composite is at its 90%-ile since Dec 1989 on a blend of P/B & P/E. That said, Quality ranks best (1 of 17) in our framework. In the US, the factor also appears expensive this month, at 55%-ile since Dec 1989 and also ranks best at 1 (of 17).

After a strong 2023, when MSCI Europe gained 20% and MSCI US 27%, January had a muted beginning with Europe ending flat and the US gaining 1.6%. FOMC Minutes indicated restrictive policy adding market expectations of rates remaining high for longer than anticipated (US Economics: FOMC Minutes: Not Cutting Soon, 3 Jan 2024). Value gained vs. Growth on the back of this at the start of January. December payrolls came in higher than expected but inflation came in line with our economists' expectations. Sticky services inflation remains in focus along the journey to target rate (Friday Finish – US Economics: The Market Is Getting Ahead of Itself, 12 Jan 2024). The data coming in through January showed continued strength with tight labour market, lower jobless claims and strong retail sales. Real GDP also rose 3.3% (annual) in the fourth quarter, higher than expected. Our economists expect the rate cuts to start from June at 25bps (US Economics: January FOMC: Definitively Neutral, 31 Jan 2024). In Europe too, ECB remained on hold as expected (ECB Reaction: No Change, Next Stop March, 25 Jan 2024) with services inflation remaining sticky. The overall buoyant economic data in January has boosted the performance of momentum trade during the month and led to relatively muted performance in Quality factor, and the sharp change in market expectation in interest rate path supported the outperformance of Low Vol factor.

# REGIONAL SUMMARIES - Factor ranking by dimension - subgroup

## Our factor ranking by region

Exploring the 4 dimensions framework across regions, in Exhibit 45 we present the current ranking of our 17 factor groups. Overall we can see that Composite Quality, Composite Momentum and Defensive Value are screening well while Size (Small Cap) and Composite Growth rank poorly.

Exhibit 45: Region specific factor ranking by dimension - subgroup
![](images/5654ff07f7512f27651b8cc1e7d7d1e6032e99548c1b184e9e2ee5446854da23.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research. The factor performances are defined as the long/short returns of the top versus bottom quintile of their respective universes, the portfolios are rebalanced quarterly and returns are equal weighted.

As can be seen in Exhibit 46 to Exhibit 50 , we compare main factor rankings by region with their key drivers:

## Defensive Value

Defensive Value ranks well across most regions. The key drivers for the strong ranking of Defensive Value are Strong Efficacy, Cheap Valuation and mostly Favorable Macro Backdrop. It ranks weaker in the US, UK and Europe owing to higher price z-score, signalling it is overbought since the last 1Y and due to its weaker performance over macro regimes. We continue to favour this factor for both regions given the outlook for higher for longer rates in both regions where yield factors can benefit.

Exhibit 46: Defensive Value Rankings - Key Drivers
![](images/0afd83a06e8e0d80d90e85f8b2789fb3a44dc86204569c8b3048e8f954823c3f.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

## Revisions

Revisions ranks weak across regions. It ranks 8th or weaker in all regions except Japan. The weak rankings come from weak macro backdrop, weaker LT efficacy and expensive valuation across most regions.

Exhibit 47: Revisions Rankings - Key Drivers
![](images/9c589cc5c6ad7941bc67f4069d28aa2d95b1db4dca3776ab477b266043e8d701.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

## Composite Quality

This is another strong factor in our framework, ranking at or below 8 for most regions. However, it does not rank as strongly in Global EM and Japan due to expensive valuation and/or unfavourable macro exposure.

Exhibit 48: Comp. Quality - Key Drivers
![](images/329c84b741deee85f98fe07cb9a00dfe0c7f82f67af21cbf508f13219486efec.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research.

## Composite Growth

Growth screens poorly in all regions except Europe. It is driven by weaker efficacy, expensive valuation, negative sentiment and negative macro exposures.

## Size (Small)

In most regions Size (Small) ranks well (except Global EM, Europe and Japan), within top 7. Most macro indicators from our Dimensions framework are currently supportive for Small caps. However, in Global EM, Europe and Japan it ranks poorly due to low efficacy, expensive valuation and mixed macro backdrop.

The table shows the factor performance relative to the Economic Cycles regimes between 1990 and 2020. The factor performances are the long/short returns of the top versus bottom quintile from MSCI US. The portfolios are rebalanced quarterly and returns are equal weighted. \* indicates lower values are preferred.
The Economic cycle is derived from the US Composite leading indicator from the OECD. The regimes are defined from the level and the change of the signal. (Expansion: Level>=100 and Change >=0; Slowdown: Level>=100 and Change <0; Recovery: Level<100 and Change >=0; Recession: Level<100 and Change <0)

Source: OECD, FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

## Quant-Driven Sector Recommendation

As of end-January 2024, our systematic sector rotation framework (Quantitative Equity Research: Rotation With Traction – Launching Our Systematic Sector Rotation Framework, 6 Jul 2023) prefers and disfavours the following sectors in the two regions:

## US

• (+) Communication Services: tailwind from Consumer Confidence Index and improving profitability in terms of gross profit-to-assets

• (+) Materials: tailwind from ISM and high dividend yield

• (–) Utilities: headwind from sentiment implied by ISM and deteriorating profitability in terms of gross profit-to-assets

• (–) Health Care: headwind from sentiment implied by ISM and weak residual momentum

## EUROPE

• (+) Consumer Discretionary: tailwind from sentiment implied by Economic Sentiment Indicator and strong free cash flow

• (+) Consumer Staples: tailwind from sentiment implied by Copper price and strong free cash flow

• (–) Health Care: headwind from sentiment implied by 10-year government bond yield and weak FCF yield

• (–) Materials: headwind from sentiment implied by PMI and weak residual momentum

## STOCK RATINGS

Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy, Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell. Investors should carefully read the definitions of al ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone. In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice. An investor's decision to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

## Global Stock Ratings Distribution

## (as of January 31, 2024)

The Stock Ratings described below apply to Morgan Stanley's Fundamental Equity Research and do not apply to Debt Research produced by the Firm.

For disclosure purposes only (in accordance with FINRA requirements), we include the category headings of Buy, Hold, and Sell alongside our ratings of Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight. Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy, Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold, and sell but represent recommended relative weightings (see definitions below). To satisfy regulatory requirements, we correspond Overweight, our most positive stock rating, with a buy recommendation; we correspond Equal-weight and Not-Rated to hold and Underweight to sell recommendations, respectively.

## Analyst Stock Ratings

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (o industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months

## Analyst Industry Views

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.
